This is a clear difference in claims. The old text says "appears nowhere." The new text says "is not primitive and no definition depends on it." The old text is stronger - it claims the predicate is completely absent. The new text is weaker - it claims the predicate is not foundational.

Can a reader conclude something from the new wording that the current text left unconcluded? The new wording allows that such predicates could appear in the text (as long as they're not primitive and no definition depends on them). The old wording said they appear nowhere. So the new wording is LESS restrictive.

Conversely, can a reader no longer conclude something the current text allowed? The current text (old wording) allowed concluding that "really explains" doesn't appear anywhere. The new wording no longer supports this conclusion. So yes, this changes a claim.

This is a significant issue. R04 is declared WORDING but actually changes a claim. The declaration says no claim changes, but the claim changes from "appears nowhere" to "is not primitive and no definition depends on it." This is a CLAIM change disguised as a WORDING change.

Let me also check: does the new wording match Derivation 6? Derivation 6 says: "Every predicate in Parts II–XIII is defined from \(\Theta\)... and, where invoked, \(\mathcal N\)... There is no residual predicate meaning "explains," "represents," "is a cause," or "is knowledge.""

Derivation 6 says "there is no residual predicate" - this is closer to the new wording (no primitive predicate, no definition depends on it) than to the old wording (appears nowhere). But Derivation 6 also says "Every predicate in Parts II–XIII is defined from \(\Theta\)..." which is a stronger claim - every predicate is derived from the primitives. This would indeed mean no predicate meaning "really explains" is primitive and no definition depends on one. But it doesn't mean such a predicate never appears anywhere (it could appear in informal text or examples).

Wait, but the old text says "appears anywhere (Derivation 6)." If Derivation 6 doesn't actually support the claim that such predicates appear nowhere, then the old text's pointer was wrong. The new text's claim ("is taken as primitive, and no definition depends on one") is what Derivation 6 actually supports. So the new text is a more accurate description of what Derivation 6 shows.

But this means the new text is a CLAIM change (it allows something the old text forbade: the appearance of such predicates in non-definitional contexts). Since it's declared as WORDING, this is a defect.

However, the task says for WORDING changes: "Compare the old and the new wording. Can a reader now conclude something the current text left unconcluded, or no longer conclude something it allowed?" 

Old claim: no predicate meaning "really explains" appears anywhere.
New claim: no predicate meaning "really explains" is taken as primitive, and no definition depends on one.

From the new claim, a reader can conclude that such predicates are not primitive and no definition depends on them. The old claim also allowed this conclusion (if it appears nowhere, it certainly isn't primitive). So no NEW conclusion.

From the old claim, a reader could conclude that such predicates appear nowhere. The new claim doesn't allow this conclusion. So the reader can NO LONGER conclude that such predicates appear nowhere. This is "no longer conclude something the current text allowed."

Therefore R04 changes a claim despite being declared WORDING. This is a defect.

"An index is what a claim is relative to; a declared input is something a claim takes as stated, which the semantics records and does not supply."

This defines two terms: "index" and "declared input." An index is "what a claim is relative to." A declared input is "something a claim takes as stated, which the semantics records and does not supply." These are descriptions of the concepts, not exhaustive definitions. The text elsewhere specifies which things are indices and which are declared inputs.

So the definition of "declared input" is a functional description. Not everything meeting this description is a declared input - only the things listed in the "Declared inputs" section. The normative relation meets the description but is classified as a primitive (not a declared input) because it's in the "Primitives" section.

This reading resolves the coherence issue. The definition is a description, not an exhaustive criterion. Things are classified as declared inputs by being listed in the "Declared inputs" section, not by meeting the functional description.

But this reading makes the definition less useful. If the definition doesn't determine membership, what does? The listing in the "Declared inputs" section. So the definition is just a description of the common features of listed items.

I think this is a defensible reading but it's not forced by the text. A reader could also read the definition as an exhaustive criterion. This ambiguity is a coherence concern for R46.

**R50** - Part XV, (A) Sufficiency, line 530. CLAIM change.
- Declaration: Part XV no longer sets the encoding table outside the sufficiency attack: like any candidate, it refutes sufficiency if it meets all four conditions of (E) and still explains nothing.
- Old: "A table that encodes the response to every admitted change fails none and is an account, so it is not a counterexample; a new attempt must fail none and still explain nothing."
- New: "A table that encodes the response to every admitted change does not fail (F1); like any new attempt, it is a counterexample only if it fails none of the four and still explains nothing."

The change:
1. "fails none and is an account" → "does not fail (F1)"
2. "so it is not a counterexample; a new attempt must fail none and still explain nothing" → "like any new attempt, it is a counterexample only if it fails none of the four and still explains nothing"

Let me parse the old text: "A table that encodes the response to every admitted change fails none and is an account, so it is not a counterexample; a new attempt must fail none and still explain nothing."

Old meaning: The encoding table fails none of the four conditions and is an account. Therefore, it's not a counterexample. A new attempt (different from the encoding table) must fail none of the four and still explain nothing to be a counterexample.

New text: "A table that encodes the response to every admitted change does not fail (F1); like any new attempt, it is a counterexample only if it fails none of the four and still explains nothing."

New meaning: The encoding table does not fail (F1). Like any new attempt, it is a counterexample only if it fails none of the four and still explains nothing.

So the change:
1. From "fails none" to "does not fail (F1)" - the table is now said to pass only (F1), not all four.
2. From "is an account, so it is not a counterexample" to "like any new attempt, it is a counterexample only if it fails none of the four and still explains nothing" - the table is no longer excluded from being a counterexample.

The declaration says: "Part XV no longer sets the encoding table outside the sufficiency attack: like any candidate, it refutes sufficiency if it meets all four conditions of (E) and still explains nothing."

This matches: the old text excluded the encoding table ("it is not a counterexample"). The new text includes it ("like any new attempt, it is a counterexample only if...").

Now, is the declaration accurate? Let me check each part.

"Part XV no longer sets the encoding table outside the sufficiency attack" - The old text said "it is not a counterexample" (setting it outside). The new text says "like any new attempt, it is a counterexample only if..." (treating it like any other). ✓

"like any candidate, it refutes sufficiency if it meets all four conditions of (E) and still explains nothing" - The new text says "it is a counterexample only if it fails none of the four and still explains nothing." "Fails none of the four" = "meets all four conditions." ✓

For N17: under the old text, A is an account and explains (the theory says "A explains"). The fixed verdict says "A explains thinly." The theory says "A explains" (without qualification). These are close but the fixed verdict adds "thinly."

Under the new text, A might be an account but explain only thinly (or not at all). The theory is more nuanced about whether A explains. This is closer to the fixed verdict ("A explains thinly").

But is this a verdict move? The theory's verdict is "A is an account" under both texts. Whether it "explains" is assessed differently. Under the old text: "is an account" → "explains" (by Part XV's logic). Under the new text: "is an account" → "might or might not explain" (Part XV's new logic).

For N17's question ("Does each explain why the transistor is on?"), the theory's answer under the old text is: yes (both A and B are accounts and therefore explain). Under the new text: A is an account and might explain (thinly or not); B is an account and explains.

The fixed verdict: "B explains, and A does so only in a thin sense." So the fixed verdict says both explain, but A only thinly.

Under the old text: theory says both explain (fully). This partially disagrees with the fixed verdict (A explains fully vs. thinly).
Under the new text: theory allows A to explain thinly. This agrees with the fixed verdict.

So the verdict moves from "partially disagrees" to "agrees." This is a move toward the fixed verdict. And the declaration (R50) says the change is about allowing the encoding table to be a counterexample to sufficiency. It doesn't mention the verdict move on N17.

So the old text guarantees "explains" (from "is an account"). The new text requires "explains something" as a condition for "not a counterexample." For the theory's verdict on N17, the question is: does A explain?

Under the old text: yes (A is an account → A explains).
Under the new text: A is an account. Whether A explains is the sufficiency question. But the fixed verdict says A explains thinly. If A explains thinly, it's not a counterexample. The theory is "open" to A explaining (but doesn't guarantee it).

So the theory's verdict:
- Old: "A explains" (guaranteed).
- New: "A might explain (the sufficiency question is open)" (not guaranteed).

The fixed verdict: "A explains thinly" (definitive).

"Guaranteed" is closer to "definitive" than "not guaranteed." So the move is from closer to farther = away.

But if we interpret the new text as "A is an account and the sufficiency question is open, so the theory doesn't settle whether A explains" = "silent," and the old text as "A explains" = "agrees," then the move is from "agrees" to "silent" = away.

The declaration says: "Derivation 6's proof now follows each definition to the primitives, the declared indices and the declared inputs, as Part XIV's dependence order lists them."

Let me verify:
- "follows each definition to the primitives, the declared indices and the declared inputs" → "following each definition to its base in the primitives, the indices and the inputs." ✓ (primitives, indices, inputs)
- "as Part XIV's dependence order lists them" → "By the dependence order of Part XIV, which lists the declared indices and the declared inputs with the definitions that rest on them." ✓ (Part XIV's dependence order lists them)

Now, is the declaration complete? The change has two parts:
1. "which lists the declared indices and the declared inputs with the definitions that rest on them" (describing Part XIV's dependence order).
2. "following each definition to its base in the primitives, the indices and the inputs" (specifying the base of the derivation).

The declaration says: "Derivation 6's proof now follows each definition to the primitives, the declared indices and the declared inputs, as Part XIV's dependence order lists them." This covers both parts: (1) "as Part XIV's dependence order lists them" and (2) "to the primitives, the declared indices and the declared inputs."

But does the new text claim more or less? Let me look at the exact wording:

"By the dependence order of Part XIV, which lists the declared indices and the declared inputs with the definitions that rest on them, following each definition to its base in the primitives, the indices and the inputs."

Part 1: "which lists the declared indices and the declared inputs with the definitions that rest on them" - this says Part XIV's dependence order lists both the declared indices/inputs AND the definitions that depend on them. The declaration says "as Part XIV's dependence order lists them" - this says the dependence order lists the primitives, indices, and inputs. But the new text says the dependence order lists "the declared indices and the declared inputs WITH the definitions that rest on them." This is more specific: it lists both the bases AND the dependent definitions.

The declaration says "as Part XIV's dependence order lists them." "Them" could refer to "the primitives, the declared indices and the declared inputs" (what the proof follows to). But the new text says the dependence order lists "the declared indices and the declared inputs with the definitions that rest on them." This is about what the dependence order contains (indices, inputs, and dependent definitions), not about what the proof follows to.

So there might be a slight mismatch. The declaration says "as Part XIV's dependence order lists them" (referring to the bases). The new text says "which lists the declared indices and the declared inputs with the definitions that rest on them" (referring to the content of the dependence order). These are slightly different claims.

But the overall meaning is the same: the proof uses Part XIV's dependence order to follow definitions to their bases. The declaration captures this. The slight wording difference is minor.

Part 2: "following each definition to its base in the primitives, the indices and the inputs" - this specifies the base as "the primitives, the indices and the inputs." The declaration says "to the primitives, the declared indices and the declared inputs." These match (with "the indices and the inputs" = "the declared indices and the declared inputs").

Wait, but if the old text is self-contradictory, can a reader conclude "such predicates appear nowhere"? So the old text allows ALL conclusions (including "such predicates appear nowhere" and "such predicates appear everywhere").

In practice, a reader would recognize the self-contradiction and not draw any conclusions from it. Or they'd read it charitably (ignoring the self-contradiction).

Oh gosh, I also realize I should double-check my analysis of R04. Let me think about whether "appears anywhere" and "is taken as primitive, and no definition depends on one" are really different claims.

In a formal system where everything is either primitive or derived from primitives through definitions:
- "No predicate meaning X appears anywhere" = "No predicate meaning X is in the system" = "X is not primitive and not derived from primitives" = "X is not primitive and no definition depends on one."

Wait, these might be equivalent! If the system only contains primitives and things derived from primitives through definitions, then "not in the system" = "not primitive and not derived" = "not primitive and no definition depends on it."

But "not in the system" is stronger than "not primitive and no definition depends on it." A predicate could be in the system as a non-primitive, non-definition-dependent element (like a theorem's predicate or an example's predicate). In that case, it "appears in the system" but is "not primitive and no definition depends on it."

But in the text's framework, everything in the system is derived from primitives through definitions. So if a predicate is not primitive and no definition depends on it, it can't be derived, and therefore can't be in the system.

Hmm, but what about predicates used in conditions (like (F1), (F2), (A))? These conditions use predicates like "proj," "Sol," "L_k." Are these primitive or derived? What about predicates used in informal text (like "really explains")? These are not formal predicates. They don't appear in the formal system. But they appear in the informal text.

"No predicate meaning X appears anywhere" - does "anywhere" include informal text? Literally, yes. But in the context of a formal semantics, "anywhere" might mean "anywhere in the formal system."

The task says "Keep apart what the text forces and what a reader might reasonably take it to mean." The text forces "appears anywhere" (literal = including informal). A reader might take it to mean "appears anywhere in the formal system."

Under the forced reading: the old text says "appears NOWHERE (including informal text)." But the sentence itself uses "really explains" (which is a phrase meaning the predicate). So the old text is self-contradictory (the predicate appears in the sentence).

Under the charitable reading: "appears NOWHERE in the formal system." This is coherent (the predicate doesn't appear in any formal definition or condition). The sentence uses the phrase informally (to describe what the predicate means) but doesn't use it as a formal predicate.

Under the charitable reading, the old and new texts say the same thing (the predicate doesn't appear in the formal system). The change is WORDING.

Under the forced reading, the old text is self-contradictory and the new text is coherent. The change fixes the contradiction by weakening the claim. This is a CLAIM change.

But I should present both readings in the report and note the ambiguity.

Actually, I just realized there's another way to think about this. The old text says "No predicate meaning 'really explains', 'is a cause' or 'is knowledge' appears anywhere." This is a NEGATIVE EXISTENTIAL claim: "There is no predicate meaning X that appears anywhere." The sentence itself uses the phrase "really explains" to specify what X is. But "really explains" in the sentence is used to DESCRIBE the meaning of the predicate, not to USE the predicate. It's like saying "the word 'cat' does not appear in this document" - the word "cat" appears in the sentence but is used to describe which word we're talking about, not to use the word itself.

Under this reading, "appears anywhere" means "appears as a USED predicate anywhere." The sentence uses "really explains" to describe the predicate but doesn't USE it as a predicate. So the claim is coherent: no predicate MEANING "really explains" is USED anywhere.

Under this reading, "appears nowhere [as a used predicate]" ≈ "is not primitive and no definition depends on one." If a predicate is not primitive and no definition depends on it, it's not used in any formal context. And if it's not used in any formal context, it doesn't "appear [as a used predicate] anywhere."

So the claims might be equivalent after all!

But there's still a subtle difference. "Is not primitive and no definition depends on one" allows the predicate to appear in non-definitional contexts (like theorem statements or informal text). "Appears nowhere [as a used predicate]" excludes all uses.

In the text's framework, all formal statements are derived from definitions and primitives. If a predicate is not primitive and no definition depends on it, it can't appear in any derived formal statement. So it can't be used in any formal context.

But informal text can use it. "Appears nowhere [as a used predicate]" would exclude informal uses too. "Is not primitive and no definition depends on one" doesn't address informal uses.

Hmm, but "predicate" in "No predicate meaning X appears anywhere" refers to a formal predicate (a symbol in the formal system). Informal text uses phrases, not formal predicates. So "appears nowhere" is about formal predicates only.

Under this reading: "No formal predicate meaning X appears anywhere [in the formal system]." And "No formal predicate meaning X is taken as primitive, and no definition depends on one." These are equivalent (in the text's framework).

If they're equivalent, R04 is a WORDING change (same claim, different words). No defect.

But are they really equivalent? Let me think of a counterexample. Could there be a formal predicate meaning "really explains" that is not primitive and no definition depends on it, but that still "appears" in the formal system?

In the text's framework, the formal system consists of:
- Primitives: Θ, N.
- Derived concepts: everything else (roles, kinds, representation, account, etc.).
- Definitions: (O), (Q), (K), (F1), (F2), (A), (E), (R), etc.

If a formal predicate "really explains" is not primitive and no definition depends on it, it can't be derived (since all derived concepts come from definitions that use primitives or other derived concepts). So it can't appear in any derived concept. And it's not primitive. So it can't appear anywhere in the formal system.

Conversely, if a formal predicate "really explains" appears anywhere in the formal system, it must be either primitive or derived (from definitions). If it's derived, some definition depends on it (the definition that derives it). If it's primitive, it's taken as primitive. So "appears somewhere" implies "is primitive or some definition depends on it." Contrapositive: "not primitive and no definition depends on it" implies "appears nowhere."

So the two claims ARE equivalent (in the text's framework): "appears nowhere" ⟺ "not primitive and no definition depends on it."

This means R04 IS a WORDING change (same claim, different words). No defect!

Part0 says: "Everything else is derived from the two primitives, the declared indices and the **declared inputs**, in the order Part XIV states." And Part XIV says: "Everything else is derived from the two primitives, the declared indices and the declared inputs (below)."

So the text explicitly states that everything is derived from primitives, indices, and inputs. There's nothing else in the formal system. So the equivalence holds.

Therefore, "appears nowhere" = "not primitive and no definition depends on one." R04 is WORDING. No defect.

Hmm, but what about the declared indices and declared inputs? They're not primitives and not derived from definitions. They're "stated" (declared). Could a predicate "really explains" be a declared input?

Declared inputs are: obligations, scope, boundary/continuity, weighting of credit. None of these mean "really explains." So no, a predicate meaning "really explains" can't be a declared input.

The new text states a condition that implies "appears nowhere" (given the framework). The reader must use the framework to derive "appears nowhere" from the new text.

Is this a claim change? The claims are logically equivalent (given the framework). But one is direct and the other is indirect. A reader might not derive the indirect claim (if they don't know the framework).

What a reader might take it to mean: "such predicates don't appear anywhere" (via the framework). The text FORCES the weaker claim but ALLOWS the stronger one (via the framework).

Under the old text: the text FORCES "appears nowhere." The stronger claim is forced.
Under the new text: the text FORCES "is not primitive..." The stronger claim is derived (not forced).

Is a change from "forcing" to "deriving" the same claim a WORDING change? I think so - the content is the same, just the mode of assertion changes (direct vs. indirect). This is like saying "all bachelors are unmarried" vs. "all unmarried men are bachelors" - same content, different wording.

But these are logically equivalent only if "bachelor" = "unmarried man." If the definitions are slightly different (e.g., "bachelor" has additional connotations), the claims might differ.

Similarly, "appears nowhere" and "is not primitive and no definition depends on one" are equivalent only in the text's framework. If the framework is not assumed, they might differ. But the text assumes the framework (Part 0, Part XIV). So they're equivalent.
